The default is sent only when the caller passes none; a caller's timeout still reaches the request, because the SDK computes its own 600 s only when none is given (`messages.py:1030`) and uses a per-request value as given (`_base_client.py:605`). Connect stays 5 s, SDK retries stay at 2, and the setting is read on every call. The implementer's stub measurements reproduced with their script: 3 attempts and `APITimeoutError` after 2.77, 2.86 and 2.86 s at 0.5 s; one attempt for a normal reply; a 529 with retry-after 0.25 retried at +0.257 and +0.509 s; and the `6fa4288` adapter still on its first attempt, header 600, after 5 s. Mutants were applied to the committed adapter, run against the new file and `tests/test_model_runtime.py`, and reverted. The implementer's seven reproduced on `ece21cb` (21, 15, 1, 3, 8, 8 and 6 failed; M4 applied only where the client has `with_options`, since the test fakes have none). On the final code (74 tests) every mutant fails tests: no default sent **24**, connect equal to the value **17**, client-timeout exception removed **1**, retries disabled on this path **3**, the setting read once per process **11**, the setting overriding a caller's timeout **8**, no validation **6**, zero accepted **2**, non-finite accepted **2**, garbage raising **1**, connect pinned at 5 **2**, `max` for `min` **19**, only read bounded **19**, the client check inverted **25**, the default back to 600 **13**, a bare float sent **20**, the default skipped for a schema call **1** or above 256 tokens **1**, and the cap below removed **3**. One defect and two doc errors were found and fixed. (1) A finite setting above `threading.TIMEOUT_MAX` (9,223,372,036 s on this Linux host; `1e10`, say) passed the parser and overflowed every socket wait (`OverflowError: timestamp out of range for platform time_t`). Against a working stub the SDK raised `APIConnectionError` on all three attempts within 1.3 to 1.5 s and the stub saw no request, so every voice call answered with the authored silence and counted `provider`. The value is now capped there, as `runtime._slot_wait` caps the slot wait, and `1e10` is answered in one attempt. Three new tests (the `1e10` and `1e300` parsing cases and that stub call) fail without the cap and pass with it. (2) This entry gave the backoff as 0.375 to 1.5 s; the SDK's two delays are 0.5 s and 1 s, each scaled by 0.75 to 1.0, so 1.125 to 1.5 s (corrected above). (3) README, `agent-runtime.md`, runbook §8 and the `busy` comment said a call ends after about 5 minutes when the provider answers errors with a retry-after. That holds only when each erroring attempt answers just inside the read timeout; by the SDK source, errors answered at once with a retry-after of 60 end the call in about 2 minutes. Each now says "at most". Checked and not changed: the client-timeout exception, which mirrors the SDK's own check, keeps the evaluation harness's configured timeout (30 s in its template), and does not apply in production, where `Anthropic()` keeps `DEFAULT_TIMEOUT`; the call sites, where only node voice, inhabitant voice and intention pass no timeout; `/agent/voice` and intention, which map a provider exception to `provider` with their authored line (`server/handlers.py`, `server/intervention_api.py`, by reading); the evidence gap, since `docs/evaluation/` and `evals/` record no live voice latency; and the dated records (ADR-035's "not re-verified" note, the 2026-11-03 evaluation's "600 s"). Not changed, outside this follow-up: `NESTED_WORLDS_OPENAI_COMPATIBLE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` uses the same parser with no cap; whether a huge value breaks that adapter was not checked.
  **Owner-account review ("approve with nits", comment-only, on `7add1ee`).** No code bug; 15 of 15 mutants were killed. Done, documentation only: (1) "about 3 minutes" is the bound for a provider that sends nothing. The read timeout is the longest gap between bytes, so a reply that keeps trickling can take longer (the review measured 59.6 s in one attempt at a 0.5 s setting). `.env.example` and this entry's title now say so, as README, `agent-runtime.md` and the PR already did. A wall-clock deadline would need a follow-up, for example streaming with an elapsed-time check. (2) There is no lower limit, as for `NESTED_WORLDS_OPENAI_COMPATIBLE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`: a value below a few seconds times out real replies, which then answer with the authored silence. README, `.env.example` and `agent-runtime.md` now say so. A floor was not added, because the scaled stub tests depend on sub-second values. (3) README's connect clause now says "less when the value is smaller", as `agent-runtime.md` did.
  **Decided (2026-11-05, under the owner's delegation: "make reasonable choices on outstanding decisions").** The lower-limit question from the owner-account review. No floor; the documented warning stays. Reasons: the setting is operator-only; the sibling `NESTED_WORLDS_OPENAI_COMPATIBLE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` has no floor, so adding one here alone would be inconsistent; any floor value would be a guess without live latency; and the scaled stub tests set sub-second values through the real variable. A value far too small fails loudly. Connect shrinks with it, so nothing is sent or billed. Every voice call then answers with the authored silence, logs `voice_fallback ... reason=provider exception=APITimeoutError` at ERROR, and fails `voice-health`. The quieter risk is a value of 2 to 5 s. Short replies get through; long ones (the intention's 500 tokens) time out after being sent, and the SDK retries them, so they may be billed more than once. Revisit when measured p95/p99 per voice task exists, and then add a warning or a floor below about twice p99, for both adapters together. Revisit sooner if either timeout is set below 10 s in a deployed environment, or if a misconfigured timeout goes unnoticed for more than one UTC day. An independent skeptic agent tried to refute this choice against the code first; it recommended keeping it with this trigger. Documentation only.

- **A moderation classify is one provider attempt under its 3 s timeout, so a chat held for screening frees its reader in seconds, not minutes** (`consciousness/runtime.py` (docstrings), `consciousness/anthropic_provider.py`, `tests/test_classify_deadline.py` (new), `tests/test_model_runtime.py`, `tests/test_moderation.py`, `README.md`, `docs/infrastructure/fly-deployment.md`, `docs/roadmap/beta-launch-checklist.md`, `docs/development/agent-runtime.md`). The classify passed `timeout=3.0`, but the anthropic adapter handed it to the SDK only as the per-attempt httpx timeout. anthropic 0.120.2 retries a timed-out, 408, 409, 429 or 5xx attempt twice by default (`_constants.py:10`, `_base_client.py:855-875` and `:897`), waits a `retry-after` of up to 60 s (`_base_client.py:828-829`), and otherwise backs off from 0.5 s, doubling to at most 8 s, with jitter (`_constants.py:13-14`, `_base_client.py:832-839`). So one classify held a WebSocket chat reader, or a request thread, through every retry, and with it a sanctioned player's close and departure (G5's exception, #142). Measured with the pinned SDK against a local stub (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` on 127.0.0.1, a fake key, clients built before the timer; adapter `generate(timeout=3.0)` / `moderation.screen` on an ambiguous line; attempts the stub saw in brackets), before → after: 429 with `retry-after: 60` about **120 s** / about **120 s** [3] → **0.01 s** / **0.05 s** [1] (the before case was run once: 120.77 s and 120.55 s, including about 0.5 s of first SDK import); 429 with `retry-after: 1` 2.01 / 2.07 s [3] → 0.01 / 0.07 s [1]; 529 1.23 / 1.44 s [3] → 0.01 / 0.06 s [1]; 500 1.47 / 1.36 s [3] → 0.01 / 0.06 s [1]; a provider that accepts the connection and never answers **10.37 / 10.48 s** [3] → **3.01 / 3.07 s** [1]. Every failing screen returned a hold with outcome `error`; a clear reply still allowed (one attempt, 0.08 s). Decisions (from the #144 owner-account review's follow-up brief; each reversible): (1) **The contract is provider-neutral.** `TextProvider.generate` now documents that a caller's `timeout` allows one attempt under that timeout: the adapter must not retry or wait out a retry-after, and shared code retries nothing. It is not a total deadline: `runtime.generate` first waits up to the same timeout for a slot, and an adapter may apply it per network phase. `runtime.generate` says the same. No signature change. (2) **The anthropic adapter turns SDK retries off for a timed call only.** `messages.create` takes no per-request `max_retries` (`resources/messages/messages.py:1001-1029`, `_base_client.py:2370-2404`), so a timed call goes through `with_options(max_retries=0)`, built once under the client lock and cached beside the base client it was made from. The copy shares the base client's httpx pool, key or bearer token, profile credential provider and its token cache, base URL and headers (`_client.py:424-497`), so `configured()` still describes the client that sends the call. A replaced `_client`, as the eval harness installs, gets its own copy. A call without a timeout still uses `_get_client()` and the SDK's two retries, untouched for the voice-timeout follow-up. (3) **No retry policy in shared code.** Trade-off accepted: a classify that meets a transient 429, 5xx or timeout now fails at once and the screen holds the text with the hold line (fail closed; the player can send it again), so holds may rise while the provider is flaky. The moderation-outcome counter follow-up will show the rate. (4) **Not a hard wall-clock bound.** httpx applies the float to connect, write, pool and each read: one attempt took **6.01 s** against a stub that sent its body a chunk a second (19.4 s and three attempts before). The slot wait (up to 3 s) comes before the call, so a chat's close can wait about 3 s plus about 3 s. The docs state the measured bound and drop the "3 s per attempt, retried up to twice, about 2 minutes" claims from README's invite-gate row, runbook §6 and G5's evidence, and a new dated note follows the checklist's 2026-11-04 notes (earlier notes and CHANGELOG entries are history and stay). `agent-runtime.md` states the contract. - **A model call waits a bounded time for a provider slot, then answers in fiction without calling the provider** (`consciousness/runtime.py`, `server/moderation.py`, `server/handlers.py`, `server/intervention_api.py`, `server/observability.py`, `main.py` (docstring), `tests/test_provider_slot_wait.py` (new), `tests/test_provider_registry.py`, `README.md`, `docs/infrastructure/fly-deployment.md`, `docs/development/agent-runtime.md`, `docs/decisions/ADR-004-day-one-data-policy.md` (outcome list), `.env.example`). `runtime.generate` took the per-provider `BoundedSemaphore` with a plain `with`, so once every slot under `NESTED_WORLDS_<NAME>_CONCURRENCY` (default 8) was taken, each caller (the moderation classify for shared text, `/speak`, `/agent/voice`, Act intentions) queued without limit while holding a request thread or a WebSocket reader. With every permit held and the old `with` restored, a saturated `generate` was still waiting at a 5 s test limit and a screen at 8 s, `/speak` did not answer within an 8 s client timeout, and a chat screen did not answer within 5 s. Before this change, in #142's credential sweep (now on `main`), that wait was the one place a revoked player's socket close and departure waited without limit. The owner, Mark Weeks, delegated #142's open question on 2026-11-04 ("Use your judgement on the questions"); these decisions were made under that delegation. (1) **The wait is bounded.** A call that passes its own `timeout` waits at most that long (the classify passes 3 s). Otherwise `NESTED_WORLDS_<NAME>_SLOT_WAIT_SECONDS` applies, per provider like the concurrency setting, default **10 s**: a voice reply of a few hundred tokens takes seconds, so ten seconds lets one to two full turnovers of 8 slots clear a burst, and a deeper backlog will not clear in a time a player waits. Empty, non-numeric or non-finite values use 10, zero or negative means no wait, and the value is capped at `threading.TIMEOUT_MAX`. When no slot opens, `generate` raises the new `runtime.ProviderBusy` without calling the provider. A slot taken is released in a `finally`, on the semaphore object actually acquired. (2) **Every caller answers in fiction.** `/speak` gives the node's authored silence (`fallback_voice`), `/agent/voice` the agent's (`_agent_voice_quiet`), and an intention the line pointing to the explicit actions, all HTTP 200 with `ai: false`. Those are the lines a provider failure already got. The daily quiet line was not used: its "Try again tomorrow" is untrue of a wait measured in seconds. The screen returns a new moderation outcome, `busy` (logged `moderation_call outcome=busy surface=… ms=…`), so shared text is held with the hold line on every screened surface, as for any screen that could not complete (ADR-004 2026-10-25 amendment). (3) **Charges taken before the wait are not refunded**, as with every existing failure after the charge. The cost ledger only increments, and a refund would make retries against a saturated provider free against the per-user caps. Taking the slot before charging was rejected: a budget or cap refusal would then itself wait for a slot, and the screen reserves outside `generate`. A busy refusal therefore keeps its model action and, on a voice surface, its voice-budget call, or for a screen its moderation classify reserve. (4) **`busy` is a voice failure, not a limit**: it logs at ERROR, is counted, and makes `python main.py voice-health` exit 1. The default would have been a limit, but the code argues against it. A voice call passes no timeout, so on the default adapter a hung provider holds its slots for the SDK's 10-minute read timeout, retried twice, and `busy` is then the only outcome recorded. A classify that times out during the call is already the failure `screen`; one that times out waiting for a slot should not pass. And the limits are deliberate stops (daily budget, per-user cap, kill switch), while `busy` is a player turned away for capacity. A pure load burst therefore fails `voice-health` too. Its output names `busy`, and the runbook says to check the provider, then raise the limit. Moving `busy` from `VOICE_FAILURES` to `VOICE_LIMITS` reverses this in one line. Noted, not changed: the Anthropic SDK's default `max_retries=2` lets one classify make three 3 s attempts with backoff between them, longer when the provider sends `retry-after` (honoured up to 60 s), so a held chat reader still waits for the call itself. Changing that needs a provider-neutral retry setting on the adapter protocol. ADR-004's descriptive list of `moderation_call` outcomes now names `busy`; its rule that shared text is held when classification raises already covered it. **32** new tests (40 after the reviews below) saturate the real semaphore by holding every permit. Blocking calls run on a daemon thread with a join limit, so the old unbounded wait fails a test instead of hanging the suite. They cover: busy within the wait and no provider call; the call's own timeout bounding the wait under a 60 s default; a slot that opens mid-wait being taken; an unsaturated call unchanged, the provider receiving the caller's timeout; a failing call releasing its slot; the charges standing; **16** cases of the wait setting and its per-provider name; the real 3 s screen holding with `busy` (that test's call took 3.05 s); `/speak`, `/agent/voice`, an intention and a WebSocket chat each answering within the bound, counting `busy`, writing no row and leaving every permit free; and `voice-health` exiting 1 on `busy`. The fake gate in `test_generate_waits_on_the_selected_providers_limit` now offers `acquire`/`release` instead of `__enter__`/`__exit__`. Each mutant, run against the new file, failed it: the old `with` acquire **9** failed, `busy` as a limit **2**, the screen mapping busy to `error` **2**, voice callers recording `provider` **3**, the release outside `finally` **1**, and the caller's timeout ignored **2**. **Adversarial review** (same delegation): each reported mutant was re-run against the new file and failed it (the same counts, except that removing the caller's-timeout branch whole failed **4**), and five more were tried: the slot taken before the charges (**1** failed), a busy screen hold recorded as `screen` (**1**), the daily quiet line on `/speak` (**1**), no wait at all (**4**), and releasing a freshly looked-up semaphore instead of the one acquired. That last mutant passed all 32 tests although the code relies on it: after a concurrency change mid-call it raises "released too many times" over a good reply and leaves the old semaphore a permit short for the callers still waiting on it. A new test pins it. The review also found `_slot_wait_env` reading `NESTED_WORLDS__SLOT_WAIT_SECONDS` for a blank adapter name, which `_semaphore_for` keys to the default provider's slots; it now reads the default provider's setting (test added). The intention path was tested only by calling `_propose` directly, so two endpoint tests with a real player key were added: `/interventions/commit` answers `_UNSETTLED` with HTTP 200, and `/speak` keeps that key's model-action and voice-budget charges. Neither the key nor the player's words reach the logs in either test. Docs: ADR-004's outcome list names `busy`, and `NESTED_WORLDS_OPENAI_COMPATIBLE_SLOT_WAIT_SECONDS` has a row beside that adapter's concurrency row (README, `agent-runtime.md`, `.env.example`). Checked and kept under the delegation: `busy` as a failure (anthropic 0.120.2 defaults to a 600 s read timeout and `max_retries=2`, so the hung-provider window is real); no refund (it matches every post-charge failure, though under sustained saturation each refusal still spends one call of the global voice budget, 500 a day, so a long outage with retries can end the day's voice early, as provider errors already can); and the 10 s default. `main` with #142 and #143 was merged in (`867738e`): the README Beta-hardening row takes `main`'s row and re-applies this change's semaphore and voice-health clauses, and the CHANGELOG keeps every entry; `main.py`, `server/handlers.py`, ADR-004 and `fly-deployment.md` merged cleanly.
  **Owner-account review ("approve with nits", on `45e4d37`).** No code bug. Done: (1) the provider-screening exception that #142 wrote into README, runbook §6, G5 and the checklist now states the bounded slot wait and the measured worst case, a classify held for about 2 minutes when the provider answers 429 with retry-after 60, and drops the "until it merges" wording; (2) runbook §8 says that a busy or failed screen on chat, Ideas, a profile or puzzle text writes one INFO `moderation_call outcome=...` line and bumps no counter, so `voice-health` does not see saturation that only reaches those surfaces; (4) this entry's stale #142 wording and test count are corrected; (5) the long `server/moderation.py` docstring line is rewrapped; (6) a parametrized endpoint test pins the busy hold on Ideas and published profiles (422, `held`, nothing published, no provider call, every slot back); (7) the two surviving mutants are killed: a call timeout of infinity falls back to the setting, and a slot is returned when the call is interrupted by a `BaseException`. Each new test fails under its mutant (1, 1 and 2). Not changed, as follow-ups: a provider-neutral retry cap or total deadline for the classify (finding 1; it needs an option on `TextProvider`), a moderation-outcome counter (finding 2), and refunding charges on a busy reply (finding 3; each busy voice reply still spends one global voice call, bounded by the 20/min per-IP limit; the spend ceiling, E2, is the owner's). On 2026-11-04 the owner, Mark Weeks, confirmed all three choices made under the delegation ("Confirmed on all three", written in the working session with the implementing agent, not on GitHub): the slot-wait bound (the call's own timeout, else 10 s), `busy` counted as a voice failure, and no refund of charges taken before the wait, so refunding a busy reply is no longer a follow-up.

- **Player-name uniqueness and the reserved names now hold for non-ASCII text** (`persistence/__init__.py`, `persistence/sanctions.py`, `agents/identities.py`, `server/handlers.py`, `server/websocket.py`, `main.py`, `docs/decisions/ADR-004-day-one-data-policy.md`, `docs/infrastructure/fly-deployment.md`, `tests/test_name_key.py`, `tests/test_sanctions.py`, `tests/test_agent_identities.py`). ADR-004 §7 makes names unique case- and whitespace-insensitively, but the mint and `/register` check compared Python's `str.lower()` of the new name with SQLite's `lower(trim(name))` of the stored ones, and SQLite's `lower()` folds ASCII only. The check was asymmetric: with `élodie` registered, `Élodie` was refused, but with `Élodie` registered, `élodie` was accepted, and the ASCII-only UNIQUE index (migration 0011) let it in, an impersonation path. A composed and a decomposed `Élodie`, which look identical, were always two names. The reserved-name checks compared `.lower()` against ASCII sets, so a fullwidth `Ｓｃｏｕｔ`, `Cartographer-⑨` or `The Locksmith` with a no-break space passed. Copilot found the first while reviewing the sanction target lookup in #138. One key, `persistence.name_key` (trim with `str.strip()`, then Unicode's compatibility caseless match, D146: NFD, `casefold`, NFKD, `casefold`, recomposed as NFKC, then trim; the first version ran NFKC, `casefold`, NFKC, see the review follow-up below), is now the comparison on every path that claims or checks a name: invite mint (`main.py invite mint` through `mint_invite_key`), redemption at `/register` (`redeem_registration_token`), the reserved names (`identities.canonical`, used by the mint and play CLIs and `POST /agent/voice`; `handlers._RESERVED_PLAYER_NAMES`, used by `/register` and `_normalize_client_name`; the keyless WebSocket join) and the sanction target lookup. `invite create` carries no name and `invite rotate` keeps the row's name, so neither changed. Uniqueness compares the key against every registered name, revoked ones included, inside one BEGIN IMMEDIATE (redemption is immediate from its token read), so two concurrent claims cannot both pass; `invite_keys` holds one row per invited player (a cohort of about twelve), so the full read is cheap. The UNIQUE index stays as the ASCII backstop; no migration. Stored names stay exactly as typed (a decomposed name stays decomposed) and no row is rewritten; the key is computed at each comparison and never stored. It is never truncated: the 32-character cap stays on the displayed name, and NFKC can expand one character (U+FDFA) to 18. `casefold` maps `ß` and `ẞ` to `ss`, so `Straße`, `Strasse` and `STRASSE` are one name and `Teßera` is the cast name Tessera. ASCII compares exactly as before (`strip().lower()`). The sanction lookup moved to the key, so the operator can type a name in any case or form, and a name that two players already share by key, possible only in a database written before this check, is refused as ambiguous, listing each spelling with `ascii()` and its participant id where one exists, and writing nothing: exact spelling never picks one, because a composed and a decomposed spelling look the same on the operator's screen. Every other read of a name is by credential, so such a pair keeps working under its own spellings. A second, local-dev-only bypass closed with the same key: the keyless WebSocket join trimmed, then capped at 32 without trimming again, so `Tessera` followed by 25 spaces and more text joined as `Tessera` plus spaces (the review follow-up also trims the shown name again). Out of scope: NFKC does not fold confusable letters from other scripts (Cyrillic `а` for Latin `a`) or remove invisible format characters (a zero-width space; the key removes them since the 2026-11-04 decisions below). Open question for the owner at the time, decided on 2026-11-04 (below): NFKC compatibility folding goes beyond ADR-004 §7's "case- and whitespace-insensitive" wording (fullwidth, circled, mathematical and ligature forms become the same name as their plain letters, and `casefold`, unlike `lower`, makes `ß` the same as `ss`); it was implemented with ADR-004 unchanged, and ADR-004 now records it. Revert checks: with the five source files at the base (`328c043` when run; its code is identical to `bed921c` on `main`, into which #139 was squashed) and the new tests kept, 40 of the 44 new or changed tests failed, the four concurrent-claim cases among them, each registering both names (four tests stop there on the missing `name_key` before any behaviour: the three key-unit tests and the `ß` test). The four that pass at the base are the two lower-case-first twin orders, the stored-spelling test and the seeded-duplicate robustness test. Swapping only `name_key` back to `strip().lower()` failed 31 (the composed, decomposed, fullwidth, `ß` and reserved-form cases; a Python-side scan already folds `É`). Dropping only the BEGIN IMMEDIATE failed exactly the 4 concurrent-claim tests, each registering both names. In a scratch check (not committed), the key is idempotent over all 1,112,064 non-surrogate code points, alone and between letters, under Python 3.11's Unicode 14.0.0. Review follow-up (an independent adversarial review, commit `0fa2c4b` (`0fa2c4b` before the rebuild onto `main`; same code)): (1) The first key ran NFKC before the first fold. Compared with Unicode's compatibility caseless match (D146) over every non-surrogate code point, it agreed alone, between two letters and after a letter, but before U+0345 U+0313 six D146 classes (18 code points: alpha, eta and omega with tonos, oxia or varia, both cases) each split into two keys. NFKC composed `ά` + U+0345 into U+1FB4 past the U+0313, folding U+1FB4 gave `ά` + `ι` and moved the U+0313 onto the iota, and the capital `Ά` has no such composite, so the case variants `Ά` + U+0345 + U+0313 and `ά` + U+0345 + U+0313 were two names. `name_key` is now D146 itself (NFD, `casefold`, NFKD, `casefold`), recomposed as NFKC and trimmed. In a scratch run (not committed) over all 1,112,064 non-surrogate code points in five placements (alone, between letters, after a letter, before and after U+0345 U+0313) it has 0 class mismatches with D146 and is idempotent; its output is unchanged from the first key for every code point alone, between letters and after a letter, and differs for 117 code points before U+0345 U+0313 and 2 after it. ASCII still keys as `strip().lower()`. Two new tests pin this: the Greek case pair through the mint CLI, and a partition check against a D146 reference over the Latin, Greek, letterlike, enclosed, ligature and fullwidth blocks in three placements. (2) The redemption's own BEGIN IMMEDIATE also keeps a registration link single-use under concurrency, and nothing tested it: the twin-name concurrency tests still pass without it, because the mint's BEGIN IMMEDIATE covers them. A new test interleaves two redemptions of one token under two names; one key is minted and the other claim reads `TokenInvalid`. Without the redemption's BEGIN IMMEDIATE both minted: one link, two keys. (3) Found while probing these paths, and also present at the base: a lone surrogate (an escaped `\ud800` in the JSON body) as the `/register` name or invite, or as a keyless body `player_name`, has no UTF-8 form, so binding it raised `UnicodeEncodeError` and the client got a 500, which the failure covenant forbids for a client-shaped mistake. `/register` now answers such a name with a 400 (`a name must be plain text`) and such an invite with the unknown-invite 403, both before the token is read, and the body-name path records the action anonymously, as it does for a reserved name. `str.isprintable()` was not used, because it would also refuse a no-break space and zero-width characters, which is the owner's question below. (4) The keyless WebSocket join now trims again after the 32-character cap, as `_normalize_client_name` does, so `Ada` followed by 29 spaces and more text joins and records `PLAYER_JOIN` as `Ada`, not `Ada` plus 29 spaces (local dev only). Confirmed and not changed then, a policy question for the owner that extends the one above (decided on 2026-11-04, below): default-ignorable characters that are not whitespace survive the key. At `/register`, `Scout` followed by U+034F (combining grapheme joiner), U+FE0F (variation selector) or U+200B, `Sc` U+00AD `out`, and U+3164 (Hangul filler) before `Scout` each registered with a 200 beside the reserved `Scout`, at the base and here, and each displays as `Scout`. Unicode's NFKC_Casefold mapping removes such characters, but removing or refusing them changes what counts as one name, so it is left to the owner; the stated-limit test and the `name_key` docstring now name them. Also not changed then, pre-existing and operator-only (both changed on 2026-11-04, below): the `invite mint` CLI has no 32-character cap, and `play --name` checks the reserved names but not registered ones. Review revert checks: with the first key formula restored, exactly 2 of the 52 tests in `tests/test_name_key.py` fail (the Greek pair and the partition check's U+0345 U+0313 placement); without the redemption's BEGIN IMMEDIATE, exactly the double-redemption test fails, with both claims registered; without the UTF-8 check and the WebSocket re-trim, exactly their 6 tests fail (a 500 where a 400, 403 or 200 is expected, and `Ada` plus 29 spaces where `Ada` is expected); with the five source files at the base, 51 of the 86 tests in the revert selection fail (the implementer's 40 plus all 11 new ones), and the same 35 pass.

  **Owner decisions (delegated, 2026-11-04).** The owner, Mark Weeks, delegated the open choices on 2026-11-04 ("Use your judgement on the questions"). These were decided under that delegation; the wording below and in ADR-004 is the implementer's, not the owner's. (1) Compatibility caseless matching is accepted. ADR-004 gains a dated "2026-11-04 name-comparison amendment" in its header; §7's "Unique at registration" bullet now says two names are the same name when their `persistence.name_key` keys are equal, that the atomic guard is that comparison under BEGIN IMMEDIATE, that the `lower(trim(name))` UNIQUE index stays as an ASCII-only backstop, that control and invisible characters are refused and the mint is capped at 32 characters, and that confusable scripts are out of scope for beta; the Status line records the amendment. Nothing else in the ADR changed. (2) Invisible characters are stripped from the key and refused at registration. `name_key` now removes every Default_Ignorable_Code_Point after the second case fold and before the NFKC recomposition, as Unicode's NFKC_Casefold does, so a stored or keyless `Scout` + U+200B, `Sc` U+00AD `out` or U+3164 `Scout` is `Scout`, and `POST /agent/voice` addressed to `Scout` + U+200B voices Scout under its canonical spelling, as a fullwidth `Ｓｃｏｕｔ` already did. The removal comes before recomposition because a combining grapheme joiner keeps a base and a mark apart: `e` U+034F U+0301 keys as `é`. `unicodedata` has no accessor for the property, so its 4,174 code points (Unicode 14.0.0, the version the pinned 3.11 implements) are hardcoded from DerivedCoreProperties.txt, with the source cited. unicode.org was not reachable from this container; the ranges were checked against the `@unicode/unicode-14.0.0` npm data package (generated from that UCD; the same 4,174 code points), and a test pins them against Unicode's own derivation rule (Other_Default_Ignorable_Code_Point + Cf + Variation_Selector, less White_Space, the interlinear and Egyptian format characters and the prepended concatenation marks) over the interpreter's character data. In a scratch run (not committed) over all 1,112,064 non-surrogate code points in the five placements above, the key has 0 class mismatches with D146 plus default-ignorable removal, is idempotent, and differs from the previous key for exactly the 4,174 default-ignorable code points in each placement; no other code point yields a default-ignorable one under NFD, case folding or NFKD. ASCII still keys as `strip().lower()`. `/register` (400, `a name must be visible text, with no control or invisible characters`) and `invite mint` refuse a name holding a control character (Cc), a default-ignorable code point, or no visible character (whitespace, default-ignorables and U+2800 BRAILLE PATTERN BLANK do not count as visible), before the reserved-name check and before the token is touched. The keyless body name is recorded anonymously, and the keyless WebSocket join is refused with a 403, as each already treats a reserved name. One consequence: a name that needs a zero-width non-joiner (some Persian spellings), an emoji joiner sequence or a variation selector (`❤` + U+FE0F, a red heart emoji) must be registered without it; the key ignores those characters anyway. Stored names are neither checked nor rewritten; the key drops their invisible characters when it compares them, and the sanction lookup's ambiguity refusal now says the names differ "only in case, Unicode form or invisible characters". (3) `invite mint` refuses a trimmed name over 32 characters (`A registered name must be 32 characters or fewer.`, nothing stored). `play --name` refuses a registered player's name in any spelling, revoked names included, when `server.moderation.writes_shared_chronicle(seed)` is true; local multi-world play and other seeds are unaffected. (4) `python main.py invite duplicates` lists registered names, revoked ones included, that share a key: each spelling through the CLI's `_shown` and as `ascii()`, its digest prefix, creation time and status. It only reads, and exits 1 if any group exists and 0 otherwise. Runbook §7 runs it before every deploy (stop and take a group to the owner; a build without the command answers `invalid choice`), and §6's sanction-name paragraph and key description name the removal and the command. Tests: `tests/test_name_key.py` goes from 58 to 105 (47 new: the table pin, removal at every position, idempotence across a removed joiner, a stored invisible-character name through the mint CLI, `/register` and the sanction lookup, four reserved spellings through `play`, 11 refused names each through `/register`, the mint CLI and the keyless WebSocket join, the keyless body name, the mint cap, `play` on and off the shared world, and `invite duplicates` with and without groups); the D146 partition test's reference now removes default-ignorables, derived by Unicode's rule in the test rather than read from `persistence`, and covers the general-punctuation block and the other default-ignorables; the stated-limit test keeps only the confusable-script case. `tests/test_sanctions.py` gains an invisible-character legacy twin refused as ambiguous with nothing written. In `tests/test_agent_identities.py`, `Scout` + U+200B moves from the unknown-name cases to the canonical-voice cases, beside `Obs` U+00AD `erver`. Revert checks, each undoing one change with the new tests kept: without the key's removal, 15 fail (removal at every position, idempotence, the stored invisible-character name, the four `play` spellings, the shared-world `play` test's zero-width spelling, the duplicates listing, the three partition placements, the sanction twin and the two voice cases; the table pin still passes, since the table is still defined); without the `/register` refusal, its 11; without the mint refusal, its 11; without the body-name check, its one test; without the WebSocket refusal, its 11 (the test reads the refusal body, because a soft-hyphenated `Scout` is also refused, with another message, by the reserved check); without the mint cap, its test; without the `play` check, the shared-world test (the off-world test is a control and passes either way); without the `duplicates` subcommand, both its tests. Not changed, and stated: a blank that is not default-ignorable is kept by the key and allowed beside visible letters, so `Scout` + U+2800 registers beside the reserved `Scout`, and confusable scripts are not folded.

(4) The `identities.canonical` docstring and the `_RESERVED_PLAYER_NAMES` comment now say the key drops invisible characters. The implementer's three questions are answered by the decisions as written, and this review keeps those answers. U+2800 beside visible letters stays allowed: the decision counts it as invisible only in a name with nothing else visible, and a trailing blank cell is a look-alike, like the confusable scripts that are out of scope for beta. Zero-width joiners and variation selectors stay refused at registration, as decided; the key ignores them, so allowing them later loosens no comparison. `invite duplicates` stays a manual, non-blocking runbook step and is not added to `scripts/deploy.sh`, because a group is permanent and an automatic block would stop every deploy after the first group. Not changed, and recorded: `/register` still accepts a name with an inner U+2028 line separator, a lone combining mark, or U+FFF9..U+FFFB. Those three format characters are not default-ignorable, and whether they render as nothing depends on the font (unconfirmed). Some stored names would be refused today, for example U+200B alone, written before the refusal. If such a name has no twin and no reserved key, no check lists it. `invite list` and `invite tokens` print names without `_shown` (pre-existing, outside this diff).

- **A revoked, rotated or sanctioned WebSocket session is closed on the server's own schedule, so a client that only listens no longer keeps receiving the world for up to about 72 s** (`server/websocket.py`, `server/protocol.py`, `server/guard.py`, `server/__init__.py`, `main.py`, `tests/test_ws_credential_sweep.py`, `tests/conftest.py`, `tests/test_sanctions.py`, `tests/test_server_rooms.py`, `README.md`, `docs/infrastructure/fly-deployment.md`, `docs/roadmap/beta-launch-checklist.md`). After #137/#138 an open socket's credential was re-checked only when a complete client frame arrived, so a socket that sent nothing kept receiving room broadcasts until the 60 s idle timeout: about 72 s with the 10 s frame deadline, over checklist G5's 1 minute. One daemon thread per process (`websocket._CredentialSweep`) now re-reads a socket's credential through the same `guard.ws_session_authorized` once its last successful check (the upgrade, a frame or a sweep) is `guard.WS_SWEEP_INTERVAL_SEC` = **5 s** old. It starts with the first open socket, ends with the last, and `server.run` stops it at shutdown. A failed or erroring read stops the writer at once, so no data frame follows, marks the session revoked and shuts the socket's read side. The reader thread wakes, sends the one 1008 close frame and leaves through the existing disconnect path: one `PLAYER_LEAVE` row, one `player_leave` broadcast. The sweep never writes a frame or takes a room lock, and its reads run outside every lock, so one stalled client cannot hold up the others. It costs at most one read per open socket per interval, none for a socket whose own frames re-checked it, and none per broadcast. A per-session latch, set only by the reader thread, keeps one close frame when the reader's own failing frame or a peer's close coincides with the sweep. A writer-side check was rejected because a quiet world would leave a revoked player listed until the idle timeout, and a thread per socket is not needed. Data frames now stop within about 5 s plus one read (WAL reads; a 5 s busy timeout at worst, and a read error closes the session); the close and departure follow within about 1 s more (owner-account review below). `tests/conftest.py` sets the interval to an hour so other suites' lookup counts and frame-driven closes stay their own; the new suite uses 0.2 s. The owner CLI's note after `sanction suspend` and `ban` now says the sessions "stop receiving the world within about 5 seconds and close within about 6 (later while a chat waits on the model provider)" (was "close within about 20 seconds"). Runbook §6, both README rows, the G5 evidence (status stays Done; the bound now meets the 1 minute criterion) and the `WS_REAUTH_INTERVAL_SEC` comment state the new bound. **16** new real-socket tests (about 14 s) passed 5 of 5 repeated runs. Revert check: with the session never registered with the sweep, **15 of 16** fail (each listen-only, keyless, race and lifecycle test times out or never sees the sweep); only the broadcast-cost bound passes either way.
  **Review pass (adversarial reviewer, branch `wk/fu-sweep-r` on `358ec03`: a code-and-test commit, a test-helper commit and this docs commit).** Every requirement was checked against the code. The mechanism held: one close frame and one leave under both forced races, no room lock or frame write in the sweep, reads outside every lock, a read error closes, and the bound runs from the last check. The implementer's revert check reproduced (15 of 16 fail with the session never registered). Mutation checks then found three guards no test exercised, and two small gaps, and the reviewer's first full-suite run exposed a sixth, test-only problem. All six are fixed. (1) With the `session.revoked` guard in `still_authorized` removed, all 16 tests still passed. Yet a session the sweep failed closed on then acts on frames the client had queued behind the frame being checked: if the reader's own read passes where the sweep's failed, a queued move is broadcast and written. A new test queues a move behind a held check and asserts no `player_move`, no `PLAYER_MOVE` row and close 1008. (2) A malformed frame queued behind the sweep's refusal turned the close into 1002. The `ProtocolError` path now sends 1008 once the sweep has ended the session (the same test, parametrized). (3) Removing the membership check in `_revoke` also passed every test. That check stops the sweep acting on a session its reader has let go of, and shutting down a socket that may be closed with its descriptor reused. A new test lands the refusal after the reader has left and asserts nothing changes. (4) The sweep's own `except` around the check survived a fail-open mutation, because `guard.ws_session_authorized` catches its own read errors today. A new test raises from the check and asserts close 1008, the exception class but not the key in the log, and the same thread still sweeping the other socket. (5) The handler looked up the module's `_SWEEP` once at join and again at leave, so a test that swapped it stranded the session and kept the old sweep's thread alive. The handler now binds the sweep once. (6) Both full-suite runs, the implementer's and the reviewer's first, failed one test the same way: a 3 s wait for the `welcome` after a good 101 (`test_shared_text_fail_closed.py` for the implementer; `test_sanctions.py::TestOpenSocket::test_suspension_closes_the_open_socket_1008_and_a_lift_lets_it_back` for the reviewer, whose machine's 15-minute load average read 0.41 just after that run). The cause was the shared helper `tests/test_day_one_recording.py::_ws_connect`, not the sweep: it read the upgrade with `recv(4096)` and kept only the status line, so a welcome that arrived in the same read was thrown away. `tests/test_movement.py` already documented this race for its own copy. The helper now reads the headers one byte at a time. A new test delays the client's first read until both have arrived. Each new test fails with its guard removed or its fix reverted. Probed, not changed: on Linux `shutdown(SHUT_RD)` wakes a blocked `recv`, but bytes already queued, or arriving later, are still read, which is why (1) and (2) mattered. A writer stuck in `sendall` to a client that has stopped reading still delays the 1008 frame and the `player_leave`; no data frame follows, and this predates the sweep (bounded since by the owner-account review below). The same holds while a chat's reader waits in `moderation.screen` for the provider semaphore (`consciousness/runtime.py`, `generate`), which has no acquire timeout. The writer is already stopped, so the revoked client receives nothing, and the post-screen check refuses the chat once the reader returns. But the 1008 close, the `player_leave`, the `PLAYER_LEAVE` row and the room listing wait for a permit; that wait is bounded only by the timeouts of the calls ahead of it, not by the sweep interval (Codex review, raised for the owner). The suite is now **21** tests (about 18 s).
  **Merged with `main` after #139 and #140.** #139 had already fixed the same `_ws_connect` race in `tests/test_day_one_recording.py` (`c821d08`, byte-wise header read, with a deterministic socketpair test), so the merge takes `main`'s helper and drops this branch's duplicate real-server test; finding (6) above is therefore already on `main`, and this batch no longer touches that file. The only other conflict was this file (this entry kept above #139's). The checklist's preamble and its dated verification notes now say G5 was updated again for the sweep and that the 72 s follow-up #138 named is done. The irreversibility verdict below is unchanged: the merge adds nothing but `main`'s own commits and this documentation.
  **PR review (Copilot on #142, high).** Confirmed, and fixed. If the sweep refused the session while the reader was inside the fresh read that clears a move or chat, the reader's earlier-started read still passed, and the move or chat was broadcast and written after the refusal. `still_authorized` now re-reads `session.revoked` after a passing lookup, and the sweep's verdict wins. The new `test_a_refusal_during_the_actions_own_check_wins` holds the reader inside that read while the sweep refuses (its own read sees the key revoked, or fails closed on a read error, as a Codex review under the owner's account probed), then lets the reader's read pass. It runs three cases: move and revoked, chat at its post-screen check and revoked, and move with a read error. All three fail without the re-read (a `PLAYER_MOVE` or `PLAYER_CHAT` row and a broadcast) and pass with it, 3 of 3 runs. Ruff clean; the sweep, lifetime, sanctions, rooms, protocol, conformance, day-one and shared-text suites gave **334 passed**. This narrows the same check-then-write gap noted in the limitations; it does not close a refusal that lands after the check returns.
  **Owner-account review (request changes on #142); all four findings fixed.** (1) Medium, reproduced: a revoked client that never reads left its writer blocked in `sendall` holding the send lock, and the reader's close waited for that lock (the 60 s socket timeout) and then for its own close frame, so the player stayed listed with no `player_leave` or `PLAYER_LEAVE` row. At the production 5 s interval the departure came at **119.8 s** here, and at **5.7 s** with the fix. `_Session.close` and the echo of a client's close now wait at most `_CLOSE_GRACE_SEC` = **1 s** for the send lock and the frame together, then shut the socket down (`SHUT_RDWR`) without a close frame, which ends the writer's `sendall` at once. Added beyond the reviewer's fix: a reader answering a protocol ping behind the stalled writer is not woken by `SHUT_RD` either (departure at **120.5 s** with only the bounded close, **5.4 s** with this), so the sweep now shuts down a refused socket whose reader still holds the session a grace after the refusal while the send lock is taken (`_release_stuck`); with the lock free it waits, so a reader waiting for the provider is left as it was. Every close path still sends at most one close frame and records one departure, and nothing follows a close frame or a data frame cut short. (2) Low: an exception outside the read killed the sweep thread, and `add()` kept trusting the dead thread. Each session's check is now wrapped (exception class logged, never the key; that session revoked; the read's own `try` folds into it), and `_run`'s `finally` lets the next socket start a new thread. (3) Low: README (both rows), runbook §6 (both paragraphs), G5, its dated note and the CLI note now say data stops within about 5 s and the close and departure follow within about 1 s more, with one exception, an open owner decision not fixed here: a chat whose screening waits for the provider semaphore (`consciousness/runtime.py` `generate`, no acquire timeout) receives nothing and is refused afterwards, but its close and departure wait for a permit. (4) Nits: a pin that the shipped `WS_SWEEP_INTERVAL_SEC` (read from source, since `tests/conftest.py` overrides it) is at most 10 s and matches the CLI note, whose close figure is it plus the grace; a test at the real 2 s throttle that a ping read after the refusal gets no pong and closes 1008; this Verified line; the checklist note's date and preamble wrap. Known limitations, unchanged: the sweep reads sockets one after another, so a slow read delays the checks behind it (in review a 4 s read put another socket's close at 7.8 s), and `add()`'s `notify_all` has no failing case. **9** new tests; the sweep suite is **33** (about 32 s, 3 of 3 runs). Revert checks, one at a time: the PR head fails 8 of the 9 (the throttle test pins code that already existed); without the bounded close 3 fail, and without the bounded echo, the sweep's release, the per-session guard, the `finally` or the pre-read check 1 each; a shipped interval of 8 s or 20 s fails the pin.
  **Review of the owner-account fixes (adversarial reviewer, branch `wk/fu-sweep-2-r` on `64305ec`).** Checked against the code; races traced by reading and by running them. The bounded close held. Behind a stalled writer, three cases each left once within **1.03 to 1.12 s**, with nothing after the cut frame: a peer close at the moment of the sweep's refusal, a protocol error, and a protocol error just after a refusal. Eight refusals under a broadcast flood each ended with the close frame last and one `PLAYER_LEAVE`. No path holds the send lock after `close` or `peer_closing` returns. Every revert check above reproduced; with the per-session guard narrowed, an older test fails too, so 2. Two findings, fixed. (a) The protocol-error close had no stalled-writer test. `test_a_protocol_error_behind_the_writer_does_not_wait_for_it` now covers it and fails at `aa99cf0` and with the lock wait unbounded. (b) The exception was stated too narrowly, as a wait for a provider slot. The reader is held for the whole screening: with a slot free, a 2.5 s classify call put the departure **2.5 s** after the refusal. The classify call's 3 s timeout is per attempt, and the Anthropic SDK retries twice by default. README, runbook §6, G5 and its note now say a chat being screened by the model provider; the CLI note already did. The latch sentence above no longer says "under the send lock". Not changed, and not shown to be reachable: `_lock_for_close` calls `settimeout` after taking the lock, outside any `try`. An `OSError` there would keep the lock and, in the `finally`, skip the departure, but `settimeout` fails only on a closed descriptor and the handler's socket stays open until it returns. A sweep thread that dies inside `_next_due` leaves open sockets unswept until the next socket starts a new one, as finding (2) specified. **10** new tests; the sweep suite is **34**.
  **Owner decisions (delegated, 2026-11-04).** The owner delegated this PR's open questions. (1) The sweep interval stays **5 s**: a read costs about 0.6 ms and comes at most once per socket per interval, about 2.4 reads a second for a 12-player cohort and about 22 at 120 sockets (measured in the owner-account review), and data stops well inside G5's 1 minute. (2) G5's 1 minute is read per socket. One thread reads due sockets in turn; a database locked long enough to hit the 5 s busy timeout on every read is an incident in which everything degrades, and it stays a stated limitation. (3) The provider-screening exception is narrowed by bounding the provider-slot wait in #144 (moderation's 3 s), not by letting the sweep finish the departure: an unbounded slot wait holds a thread on every model-backed endpoint, a saturated provider then answers in fiction as the failure covenant asks, and the reader-owned single departure that this PR's race tests pin stays as it is. The classify call's own seconds (3 s per attempt, up to two SDK retries) remain and are stated. README, runbook section 6, G5 and the checklist note now say so and name #144; until it merges the slot wait has no limit, as they also say. This decision commit changes documentation only.

  **Irreversibility check:** none — no migration, no golden re-pin, no new `world_mutations` write path (no `record_mutation` call site added or removed; the sweep writes nothing and the departure is the existing `PLAYER_LEAVE` in the reader's `finally`), no world-meta pin, no era-bank edit. The diff is an in-memory re-check thread, one constant, a close-frame latch, the reads that already authorize a socket, and CLI and doc text. No new player surface; ADRs untouched. The review's commits touch no migration, chronicle write path, pin or era bank either: a close-code choice on the existing `ProtocolError` path, the sweep bound once per socket, six tests, one test-helper read loop and this entry. The owner-account review's commits touch none of them either: a close grace, a bounded close and echo, the sweep's shutdown of a stuck refused socket and its thread's restart, nine tests, and CLI and doc text; the departure is still the one existing `PLAYER_LEAVE`. The review of those fixes adds one test and changes doc wording only.

- **Player-reachable agent walks store nothing, and voice outages reach the operator; the 2026-11-03 decision review is recorded** (`docs/evaluation/2026-11-03-decision-pushback.md`, `server/handlers.py`, `server/rooms.py`, `agents/agent.py`, `causality/wiring.py`, `persistence/__init__.py`, `server/observability.py`, `server/intervention_api.py`, `server/guard.py` (docstring), `main.py`, `scripts/beta_metrics.py`, `tests/test_live_agent_walks.py`, `tests/test_voice_outcomes.py`, `tests/test_agent_identities.py`, `tests/test_server.py`, `tests/test_m4_review.py`, `static/explorer.js`, `frontend/e2e/ux-review.spec.js`, `README.md`, `docs/infrastructure/fly-deployment.md`, `docs/roadmap/beta-launch-checklist.md`). The owner asked which decisions a qualified engineer would strongly push back on. Five dimension reviewers and five adversarial verifiers took **29** candidates to **9** significant findings and **0** fundamental; R4–R9 are recorded for the owner. Two findings were also fixed on `main` while this batch was in review, and this batch withdrew its own versions at the merges. **R1** (the gate failed open) is #137's latch. **R2**'s identity half is #139's server-owned names: this batch's `NESTED_WORLDS_INVITE_GATE` mode, revoke guard, `RESERVED_NAMES`, `/agent/voice` presence rule and trace lookup are gone. What remains of R2 is the writes. On `d15ed9f` one `GET /agent` with a 20,000-character name wrote **1,137** rows and **23,871,488** bytes; on `main` it still ran a fully wired walk as Scout and saved runs and memory, and `/observe` still recorded its visits and broadcast `causal_event`s. Now `/agent` is a simulation and `/observe` is a live walk streamed to its watcher: neither writes chronicle rows, ripple, overlays, agent runs, agent memory or puzzle pins (`Agent.pin_puzzles`), and `/observe` broadcasts presence only. Walks take turns on the one Observer presence (`Room.observer_lock`). The turn is held only while the walk is computed, and the stream is written after it is released. Presence moves only at the walker's stops (at most 40, 10 ms apart), so no player's outbox floods. A watcher who cannot get a turn within 2 s is told so, and the explorer now says the Observer is busy. Explorer Observe walks therefore no longer leave history, a visible change. **R3:** every voice failure was HTTP 200 with a WARNING and no count. Each quiet line on `speak`, `agent_voice` and `intention` now logs `voice_fallback surface=… reason=…` on `nested_worlds.voice` (ERROR for provider/db/unconfigured/screen, WARNING for budget/action_cap/disabled). `screen` is a classifier failure or unusable verdict that held shared text before the voice was asked. Moderation's own budget, per-user cap and disable switch count as `budget`, `action_cap` and `disabled`. A content decline is policy and is not recorded. Answered lines and every quiet reason except a store failure bump a `voice_outcome:` counter in the existing `cost_budget` rows, with no migration. That is at most 24 rows a day (3 surfaces × 8 reasons), surface and reason only, kept like the other `cost_budget` rows. The counter write waits at most 50 ms on a locked store and a dropped one is logged, so under write contention the counts are a lower bound (the owner's review measured 388 of 400 with 8 writer threads). On `speak` and `agent_voice` a successful line is counted after its reply is written; `intention` counts when the model answers. `python main.py voice-health` reports the counters and exits 1 on a provider, screen or model-configuration failure (limits and the kill switch pass); `beta_metrics.py` reports answered vs. quiet lines. Reviews: an adversarial review of the first commit found seven defects; Copilot and Codex on the PR found four (a 500 from the trace lookup, overlapping Observer walks, uncounted screen failures, `voice-health` passing with unusable model ids); the owner's review asked for the #139 reconciliation and found four surviving mutants, all now killed by new tests (`/observe` pinning puzzles, `ok` counted before the reply, the metrics window off by one day, a Clarification counted as a failure). A final review of the reconciled diff reproduced one regression this batch had introduced: with the walk unwired and the stream buffered, a depth-11 walk sent about 80 presence frames in about 10 ms, and every connected player was evicted (4 of 4 on HEAD, 0 of 4 on `main`). Presence now moves only at the walker's stops, and a test with a real draining `/ws` player pins it. The same review found moderation-limit holds uncounted, the explorer ignoring `busy`, and four untested paths: the turn released before streaming, `agent_voice` counting, `--probe` failing and the slow-reader case. All are fixed, and each new test was checked against its mutant. Changed tests: `test_server` and `test_m4_review` pinned the old recording, and four of #139's identity tests now assert that the walks store nothing, keeping every name assertion.
  **Verified:** Python 3.11.18, Node 20.19.0. On `d1e3199` (merged with `main` at `bed921c`, with the final review fixes; the commit after it changes only this line), `PW_CHROMIUM_PATH=/opt/pw-browsers/chromium ENFOLDED_E2E=1 ./scripts/check.sh` exited 0 (2026-11-03T15:33:07Z–15:45:07Z, 720s): Ruff passed; **2301 Python passed** (507.21s); **138 Vitest passed** (15 files); production bundle byte-fresh (`index-vRwqOYno.js`, unchanged); installed-wheel smoke verified `enfolded-0.1.1rc2` (`schema_version` **29**); Playwright **100 passed** (3.4m).   **Irreversibility check:** none — no migration (the counters reuse `cost_budget` rows under a new prefix), no golden re-pin, no world-meta pin, no era-bank edit, and no new `world_mutations` write path: the diff removes two (`GET /agent`, `GET /observe` lose their record/ripple/substance wiring) and adds no `record_mutation` call. Historical rows are untouched; closing the caller-chosen-performer paths follows the "players own their actions" covenant without rewriting any recorded actor.

- **Agent identities are server-owned; client-chosen names no longer write history** (`agents/identities.py`, `server/handlers.py`, `main.py`, `interface/__init__.py`, `tests/test_agent_identities.py`, `tests/test_server.py`, `tests/test_guard.py`, `tests/test_m4_review.py`, `tests/test_day_one_recording.py`). `GET /agent?name=` took any string, with no length cap. It walked an `Agent` on a recording bus, wrote `AGENT_VISIT` rows with that string as `data.agent`, and saved `agent_memory` and `agent_runs` under it, so `?name=Tessera` read and overwrote a cast member's memory. `GET /observe?name=` (capped at 32) entered a live room presence and wrote `AGENT_VISIT` rows under any name, a registered player's included. `POST /agent/voice` stored its `agent_name` in `AGENT_VOICE` `data.agent`. `data.agent` is not in `persistence._REDACTABLE_FIELDS` and moderation never screens it, so after launch such a string could leave only by a whole-DB restore, which is not one of the chronicle covenant's three mechanisms. Both clients turn `data.agent` in node history into Speak targets. Review found three more places the client's string reached. The walker's payload is recorded on every row its walk fires, so `DANGER_ALERT`, `PUZZLE_SOLVED`, `PUZZLE_FAILED` and `PUZZLE_REARM` rows carried the name too. When a heartbeat cast walker shared a node with an `/observe` presence, `heartbeat._hold_conversation` wrote an `AGENT_TALK` row with that name in `data.b` and as a speaker in `data.lines` (neither field is redactable), and broadcast `agent_encounter` and `agent_talk` frames. `/agent/voice` also put the string into the voice prompt as "You are <name>", where the screen never looked. The same change closes all three. The beta checklist's checked S1 box ("shared text fails closed") did not cover this path; the checklist is not edited here. `agents/identities.py` now defines the one set: the **12** cast names, the situation keeper (`multiverse.situation.KEEPER`, which is `Tessera`, already cast), `Scout` and `Observer`, **14** names in all. `/agent` always walks as Scout and `/observe` always as Observer; `?name=` is ignored. `persona` still picks only from the **4** archetypes. `persona=Mallory the Great` falls back to Scout's persona, and the string is in no row. `/agent/voice` matches a set name after trimming, case-insensitively, and stores the canonical spelling (`"  aunt entropy "` is stored as `Aunt Entropy`). A missing `agent_name` is still Scout. Any other name, including a non-string, gets the existing `_agent_voice_quiet` body (HTTP 200, `ai: false`). That happens after the place resolves, so an unknown place is still the 404, and before the screen, any budget, and any row. With the real screen and the real cost ledger, **12** unknown-name requests (11 names, plus one with `persona`) left the per-user moderation-action, per-user model-action, global voice and per-user voice counters at **0**, called neither the screen nor the stub provider, and wrote no `AGENT_VOICE` row. Each of **4** set names (`Tessera`, `  aunt entropy `, `SCOUT`, `observer`) charged each of those counters **1** and wrote one row under the canonical name. The quiet line echoes an unknown name, trimmed and capped at 32, only to the client that sent it. A stale `Recorder` trace from an old local DB reads "Recorder does not answer. …"; a blank or non-string name, or one that is not printable once trimmed (a control or format character, or a lone surrogate with no UTF-8 form), reads "Someone does not answer. …". Neither is stored, broadcast, or logged. The `/observe` test watches a real WebSocket in the room: `agent_enter`, every `agent_move`, and `agent_leave` are all named `Observer` when Bea asks for `?name=Ada`. Concurrent `/observe` walks in one world now always share the one `Observer` presence, so the first to finish sends `agent_leave` while another may still be walking. Neither client sends `?name=`, so they already behaved this way; it is not changed here. A separate commit reserves `Scout` and `Observer` from player names in `/register`, the keyless WebSocket join, keyless body names, `invite mint`, and `play`. **This extends ADR-004 §7's cast-only list.** The owner, Mark Weeks, directed the merge of #139 on 2026-11-03 and delegated its open choices; the reservation is kept and recorded as ADR-004's 2026-11-03 reserved-names amendment, which also updates §7's "Unique at registration" bullet. No production database exists yet, so no registered player holds either name; registered keys skip the reserved check, so a deployment that already had one would keep it. Names already registered are not changed. `Scoutmaster` still registers. No client change: `static/explorer.js` and `frontend/src/components/Interact.jsx` take Speak targets from `data.agent` in `/history` and send them as `agent_name`, so the cast, Scout and Observer keep working. Neither client sends `?name=` to `/observe`, and neither calls `/agent`. The operator CLI `main.py agent --name` still accepts any name for `agent_memory` and `agent_runs`. It writes no chronicle row, and it is not changed here. With `server/handlers.py` from `23b8830` and the new module in place, **8** of the first **11** new tests failed; the **3** that passed are controls (the set itself, a Tessera voice, a missing name). With `server/handlers.py` and `main.py` from the first commit, **11** of the **12** reserved-name tests failed; `Tessera`, already reserved, passed. Review follow-up, in a later commit: the `/agent` and `/observe` tests now assert every `data.agent` in the world, not only `AGENT_VISIT` rows, and the canonical-name voice test asserts that the prompt's agent block opens "You are <canonical name>, a <persona>." A handler that stored the canonical name but voiced the raw string failed **3** of those **4** cases (`Tessera` is already canonical). With `server/handlers.py`, `main.py` and `interface/__init__.py` from `23b8830`, **19** of the **23** new tests fail; the **4** that pass are the controls named above. Second review follow-up (Copilot, on `0921e71`): the quiet line hashed the trimmed unknown name for a persona, and a lone surrogate (an escaped `\ud800` in the JSON body) has no UTF-8 form, so `persona_for_name` raised `UnicodeEncodeError` before the place resolved and outside the voice `try`. Over real HTTP, `"\ud800"` alone was already a **500** at `23b8830`; 32 spaces then `"\ud800"` was a 200 there, because the old cap ran first and cut the surrogate, and a 500 at `0921e71`, because the name is now trimmed before the cap. The echo is now used only if it encodes as UTF-8; otherwise the line reads "Someone". The new test's **3** names (`"\ud800"`, 32 spaces then `"\ud800"`, `"Ada\udfff"`), each also sent with `persona` `"\ud800"`, failed with a 500 against the `0921e71` handler and now get the quiet 200 with `ai: false`, no screen, all four counters at **0**, and no row. The same review found that the shared test helper `tests/test_day_one_recording.py::_ws_connect` read the upgrade reply with `recv(4096)` and kept only the status line, so a welcome frame that arrived in the same segment was discarded and the caller timed out waiting for it. A `welcome` `TimeoutError` in `tests/test_shared_text_fail_closed.py` was reported under load in a 2026-11-02 baseline run on `23b8830`; it fits that race, but it was not reproduced here. The helper now reads one byte at a time up to the blank line. A socketpair test buffers the 101 and a welcome frame before the first read: the old helper left `b""` after the head, the new one leaves the frame. The other upgrade readers in `tests/` (`test_server_limits._upgrade`, `test_movement._connect_keep_rest`) already return the extra bytes, and `test_guard._ws_upgrade` never reads a frame. Third review follow-up (review 5745364899 from the owner's account, "approve with nits", written on `0921e71`; its surrogate, Verified-line and `_ws_connect` findings confirm Copilot's and are fixed by the second follow-up above): the quiet line's echo also returned NUL, a zero-width space (U+200B) and a BOM (U+FEFF) raw, because `strip()` keeps them and they encode as UTF-8, so `"Scout\u200b"`, which is not Scout, came back as `"Scout\u200b does not answer. …"`, the zero-width space included. The echo is now kept only if `str.isprintable()` holds, which also rejects lone surrogates, and the UTF-8 check is gone. The trim and the 32-character cap are unchanged. The parametrized test now sends **7** names (the **3** surrogate names, `"\x00"`, `"\u200b"`, `"Scout\u200b"`, `"\ufeffAda"`), each also with `persona` `"\ud800"`. Each gets the "Someone" quiet 200 with `ai: false`, no screen, all four counters at **0**, and no row. With the `cf35eba` UTF-8 check in place of the new one, the **4** new names fail and the **3** surrogate names pass; with no check, all **7** fail, the surrogates with a 500. `Élodie`, `影` and a decomposed `Zoë` (`"Zoe\u0308"`, with a combining mark) still echo. The review's two mutation survivors are now killed. One test saves `agent_memory` for `Aunt Entropy`, voices `"  aunt entropy "`, and finds the remembered place count, "You have stood at <node> before" and the logged movement in the stub prompt. The other opens the authored situation in world **382** with `situations.install`, `discover`, `choose` and `advance`, which records `SITUATION_COMMITTED` at the regulator; it voices `"tessera"` at the gallery, whose node history lacks that row, and finds "The shared decision holds: …" in the prompt. Passing the raw request to `load_agent_memory` fails only the memory test, and passing it to `keeper_history` fails only the keeper test. An independent review of this follow-up found that the keeper test's check that the gallery's history lacks the commitment could never fail: default `json.dumps` writes the curly apostrophe in the commitment line as an escape. The check now compares unescaped text and also checks the committed row's id. With the commitment text seeded at the gallery, the old check passed and the new one fails; the keeper mutation still fails the test. The `agents/identities.py` docstring no longer says that no path takes an agent name from a client. It now says no path lets a client choose a name outside the set, and that `/agent/voice` takes one only through `canonical`. The review's finding 1 (the ADR-004 §7 hunk) is settled by the 2026-11-03 amendment above; findings 7 (`main.py agent --name`, the shared Observer presence; both stated above) and 8 (checklist S1 evidence) are not changed here. **+33** tests. The suite collects **2078** at `23b8830`, **2105** at `803ad02`, and **2111** here. The #136 entry recorded **1942** from `check.sh`; that difference was not investigated.
